    Headline
    appetite stimulation, glycosuria, hypoglycemia
    General Information
    Codeine; dexbrompheniramine can exert effects on the endocrine system. During clinical use, changes in glucose utilization, glycosuria, hypoglycemia, appetite stimulation, and pheochromocytoma stimulation have been reported with antihistamine-opioid combination products that are similar to codeine; dexbrompheniramine.     Headline
    neonatal opioid withdrawal syndrome, neonatal respiratory depression, physiological dependence, psychological dependence, withdrawal
    General Information
    As with other opiate agonists, use of codeine presents the potential for abuse. Although the true prevalence of addiction to opioids in chronic pain individuals is unknown, estimates from multiple studies indicate that it may range from 0% to 50% and 0% to 7.7% in chronic non-malignant pain and cancer pain, respectively. Physiological dependence, however, will occur during chronic codeine; dexbrompheniramine therapy as evidenced by a withdrawal syndrome occurring after abrupt discontinuation of the drug. Codeine; dexbrompheniramine is indicated for short-term use; therefore, addiction and physiological dependence are not expected to occur. Symptoms of withdrawal include nausea and diarrhea, coughing, lacrimation, yawning, sneezing, rhinorrhea, profuse sweating, twitching muscles, abdominal and muscle pain/cramps, hot and cold flashes, and piloerection. Elevations in body temperature, respiratory rate, heart rate, and blood pressure may also occur. Without treatment, most symptoms resolve within 5 to 14 days; however, there appears to be a secondary or chronic abstinence syndrome which may last 2 to 6 months, characterized by insomnia, irritability and muscle aches. The shorter the onset and duration of action of the opiate agonist, the greater the intensity and rapidity of onset of withdrawal symptoms. Routine use of opioid agonists by an expectant mother can lead to neonatal respiratory depression and neonatal opioid withdrawal syndrome. Withdrawal symptoms in a newborn occur 1 to 4 days after birth and include generalized tremors, hypertonicity with any form of tactile stimuli, hyperalertness, sleeplessness, excessive crying, vomiting, diarrhea, yawning, and pyrexia. It is important to differentiate physiological dependence, the onset of a withdrawal syndrome upon abrupt discontinuation of the drug, and psychological dependence. Psychological dependence is a behavioral syndrome characterized by drug craving, overwhelming concern with acquisition of the drug and other drug-related behaviors such as drug selling and seeking the drug from multiple sources.

    Codeine; dexbrompheniramine contains dexbrompheniramine. In reported cases, counts returned to normal following discontinuation of the antihistamine.

    Headline
    adrenocortical insufficiency, amenorrhea, gonadal suppression, gynecomastia, impotence (erectile dysfunction), infertility, lactation suppression, libido decrease, libido increase, menstrual irregularity
    General Information
    Cases of adrenocortical insufficiency have been reported with opioid use, more often following more than 1 month of use. Presentation of adrenal insufficiency may include non-specific symptoms and signs including nausea, vomiting, anorexia, fatigue, weakness, dizziness, and low blood pressure. If adrenal insufficiency is suspected, confirm the diagnosis with diagnostic testing as soon as possible. If adrenal insufficiency is diagnosed, treat with physiologic replacement doses of corticosteroids. Wean the individual off of the opioid to allow adrenal function to recover and continue corticosteroid treatment until adrenal function recovers. Other opioids may be tried, as some cases reported use of a different opioid without recurrence of adrenal insufficiency. The information available does not identify any particular opioids as being more likely to be associated with adrenal insufficiency. Opioids inhibit the secretion of adrenocorticotropic hormone (ACTH), cortisol, and luteinizing hormone (LH). Monitor individuals for symptoms of opioid-induced endocrinopathy, particularly those receiving a daily dose equivalent to 100 mg or more of morphine. Individuals should seek immediate medical attention if they experience symptoms of adrenocortical insufficiency.  Long-term use of opioids may influence the hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal axis, leading to hormonal changes that may manifest as a libido decrease or libido increase, impotence (erectile dysfunction), lactation suppression, menstrual irregularity such as early menses or amenorrhea, gynecomastia, or infertility. The causal role of opioids in the clinical manifestations of hypogonadism (gonadal suppression) is unknown. It is not known whether the effects on fertility are reversible.

Description
  Text
  Codeine; Dexbrompheniramine is an oral antitussive-antihistamine combination product. These products are used to provide temporary relief from rhinorrhea, sneezing, itching of the nose or throat, or itchy watery eyes due to hay fever, upper respiratory allergies, or allergic rhinitis, and cough as may occur from throat irritants or the common cold. Dexbrompheniramine is a first-generation sedating antihistamine (H1-blocker) of the propylamine class and exhibits anticholinergic and sedative effects; excitability may occur, especially in children. Codeine is an opioid agonist relatively selective for the mu-opioid receptor and is believed to act centrally on the cough center. Avoid codeine; dexbrompheniramine use if an individual is taking a monoamine oxidase inhibitor (MAOI) or for 2 weeks after stopping the MAOI. Codeine; Dexbrompheniramine was FDA-approved in September 2025.

Mechanism Of Action
  Text
  Codeine: Codeine is a mu-opiate receptor agonist. Opiate receptors include mu, kappa, and delta, which have been reclassified by an International Union of Pharmacology subcommittee as OP1 (delta), OP2 (kappa), and OP3 (mu). The antitussive effects of codeine are mediated through direct action on receptors in the cough center of the medulla. Codeine also has a drying effect on the respiratory tract and increases the viscosity of bronchial/respiratory secretions. Cough suppression can be achieved at lower doses than those required to produce analgesia. Hypotension is possibly due to an increase in histamine release and/or depression of the vasomotor center in the medulla. Induction of nausea and vomiting possibly occurs from direct stimulation of the vestibular system and/or the chemoreceptor trigger zone.
Dexbrompheniramine: Dexbrompheniramine is an H1-antagonist belonging to the alkylamine class of antihistamines. Antihistamines appear to compete with histamine for receptor sites on effector cells. Dexbrompheniramine also has anticholinergic (drying) and sedative effects. Among the antihistaminergic effects, it antagonizes the allergic response (vasodilatation, increased vascular permeability, increased mucus secretion) of nasal tissue.

Pharmacokinetics
  Special Populations
    Name
    Hepatic Impairment
    Description
    The pharmacokinetics of codeine in people with hepatic impairment is unknown as no formal studies have been conducted.
    Name
    Renal Impairment
    Description
    Codeine pharmacokinetics may be altered in people with renal failure. Clearance may be decreased and the metabolites may accumulate to much higher plasma levels in people with renal failure as compared to people with normal renal function.
    Name
    Other
    Description
    CYP2D6 Ultra-Rapid MetabolizersSome individuals may be ultra-rapid metabolizers due to a specific CYP2D6 genotype (gene duplications noted as *1/*1xN or *1/*2xN). Ultra-rapid metabolizers of CYP2D6 convert codeine into morphine more rapidly and completely than others. Higher than expected serum morphine concentrations occur due to the rapid conversion and serious toxicity, including life-threatening or fatal respiratory depression, may occur. In a randomized, controlled trial involving 9 extensive metabolizers and 9 poor metabolizers, the percentage of the codeine dose converted to morphine and its metabolites was 3.9% and 0.17%, respectively. The prevalence of this CYP2D6 phenotype varies widely and has been estimated at 1% to 10% in Caucasian subjects, 3% to 4% in Black subjects, 1% to 2% in East Asian subjects (Chinese, Japanese, Korean), and may be greater than 10% in certain racial/ethnic groups (e.g., Oceanian, Northern African, Middle Eastern, Ashkenazi Jews, Puerto Rican).
CYP2D6 Poor MetabolizersIn a randomized, controlled trial involving 9 extensive metabolizers and 9 poor metabolizers, the percentage of the codeine dose converted to morphine and its metabolites was 3.9% and 0.17%, respectively. Approximately 7% to 10% of the Caucasian population lacks functional CYP2D6 activity.
  Text
  Codeine; Dexbrompheniramine is administered orally.
Dexbromepheniramine: Dexbrompheniramine is the pharmacologically active (S)-(+) enantiomer of brompheniramine. Antihistamines are well absorbed from the gastrointestinal tract after oral administration. Dexbrompheniramine is extensively metabolized in the liver and excreted by the kidneys. The biological half-life is approximately 25 hours.
Codeine: Codeine has an apparent Vd of approximately 3 to 6 L/kg, indicating extensive distribution of the drug into tissues. Codeine has low plasma protein binding with about 7% to 25% of codeine bound to plasma proteins. Codeine is metabolized by conjugation to codeine-6-glucuronide (70% to 80%), by O-demethylation to morphine (5% to 10%), and by N-demethylation to norcodeine (approximately 10%). Both UDP-glucuronosyltransferase (UGT) 2B7 and 2B4 are the major enzymes mediating glucuronidation of codeine to codeine-6-glucuronide. The analgesic activity of codeine-6-glucuronide is unknown. CYP2D6 is the major enzyme responsible for conversion of codeine to morphine, and CYP3A4 is the major enzyme mediating conversion of codeine to norcodeine. Morphine and norcodeine are further metabolized by conjugation with glucuronic acid. The glucuronide metabolites of morphine are morphine-3-glucuronide (M3G) and morphine-6-glucuronide (M6G). Morphine and M6G are known to have analgesic activity. Norcodeine and M3G are generally not considered to possess analgesic properties. Approximately 90% of the total dose of codeine is excreted through the kidneys, of which approximately 10% is unchanged codeine. The plasma half-lives of codeine and its metabolites have been reported to be approximately 3 hours.
 
Affected cytochrome P450 isoenzymes and drug transporters: CYP2D6, CYP3A4
Codeine is a CYP2D6 and CYP3A4 substrate. CYP2D6 is the major enzyme responsible for conversion of codeine to morphine, and CYP3A4 is the major enzyme mediating conversion of codeine to norcodeine. Affected CYP450 isoenzymes and/or drug transporters have not been identified for dexbrompheniramine.

Dosage Adjustment Guidelines
  Hepatic Impairment
  Specific dose adjustment recommendations are not available; decreased elimination of codeine is expected. Use with caution and monitor closely for signs of codeine toxicity (respiratory depression, sedation, and hypotension).
  Renal Impairment
  Specific dose adjustment recommendations are not available; however, decreased elimination of codeine is expected. Use with caution in people with moderate to severe renal impairment, and monitor closely for signs of codeine toxicity (respiratory depression, sedation, and hypotension).
